Predgovor / Preface

Predgovor

Monografija Imena u tišini nastaje kao spomen, svjedočanstvo i čin zahvale. Ona je posvećena žrtvama Domovinskoga rata čija su imena, sudbine i dostojanstvo predugo bili skriveni u tišini masovnih grobnica, ali i ljudima koji su tu tišinu imali snage, znanja i moralne odgovornosti prekinuti.

U vremenu razaranja i neizvjesnosti, Hrvatska nije samo tražila svoje mrtve nego je, oslanjajući se na čovječnost, znanost i međunarodnu suradnju, stvarala jedan od prvih sustavnih modela DNK identifikacije ratnih žrtava. U tome su posebnu ulogu imali stručnjaci iz Splita i njihovi suradnici u Hrvatskoj i svijetu, čiji je rad pokazao da, i u najtežim povijesnim okolnostima, istina može biti vraćena obiteljima, a identitet oduzetih života ponovno potvrđen.

Stoga, ova monografija nije samo puki zapis o forenzičkim postupcima i znanstvenim postignućima. Ona je svjedočanstvo o susretu čovjeka, znanosti i svijeta u službi najviše vrijednosti — dostojanstva žrtve i prava obitelji na istinu. Ujedno je i podsjetnik da je hrvatski doprinos razvoju prvih DNK identifikacija bio i stručan i povijesno važan, a njegovo je značenje daleko nadišlo nacionalne okvire.

Neka ova monografija bude trajni znak poštovanja prema žrtvama, zahvala pionirima toga rada i obveza svima nama danas da istinu čuvamo jasno, dostojanstveno i bez zaborava.

Urednici

Preface

The monograph Names in the Silence stands as a memorial, a testimony, and an act of gratitude. It is dedicated to the victims of the Homeland War whose names, fates, and dignity remained hidden for far too long in the silence of mass graves, but also to the people who had the strength, knowledge, and moral responsibility to break that silence.

In a time of destruction and uncertainty, Croatia was not only searching for its dead; relying on humanity, science, and international cooperation, it was also developing one of the earliest systematic models of DNA identification of war victims. In this effort, a particularly important role was played by experts from Split and their collaborators in Croatia and around the world, whose work demonstrated that even in the most harsh historical circumstances, the truth could be returned to families and the identities of those whose lives were taken away could be restored.

Therefore, this monograph is not merely a record of forensic procedures and scientific achievements. It is a testimony to the encounter of humanity, science, and the world in the service of the highest values—the dignity of the victims and the right of families, to the truth. At the same time, it serves as a reminder that the Croatian contribution to the development of the first DNA identifications was both professionally significant and historically important, and that its meaning extends far beyond national borders.

May this monograph stand as a lasting sign of respect for the victims, a tribute to the pioneers of this work, and a commitment for all of us today to preserve the truth clearly, with dignity, and never allowing it to be forgotten.

Editors

PDF page 5

Otvorena vrata / Open Doors

Otvorena vrata

prof. dr. sc. Željana Bašić

Na početku Domovinskoga rata bila sam još dijete, a rat je trajao dovoljno dugo da u njemu dočekam i tinejdžerske godine. Trajao bi predugo i da je trajao samo jedan dan, a ne više od 1 600 dana. Danas, nakon više od trideset godina, broj nestalih i dalje nadilazi broj 1 600 – i još uvijek ih tražimo. Sjećam se prizora s televizije koji su mi, poput strašnih vinjeta, ostali urezani u sjećanje. Tko bi tada mislio da ću desetljećima poslije pisati predgovor za ovu monografiju, koju držite u rukama?

Neke od protagonista ove knjige upoznala sam osobno, neke kroz priče, a s nekima sam s vremenom razvila i prijateljstva. Njihove se priče u ovoj monografiji pojavljuju izravno, ali i posredno, utkane u širi narativ koji nadilazi pojedinačna iskustva. Ovdje ću samo kratko izdvojiti ono što me se najviše dojmilo tijekom pripreme ovih materijala, a čitatelju prepuštam da sam prepozna što mu je u tome najvažnije. Ova monografija ne nastoji obuhvatiti sve. Da to čini, sadržavala bi na tisuće znanstvenih, povijesnih, medicinskih, forenzičkih i ljudskih priča. Ona nas prije svega podsjeća na mogućnosti jednoga znanstvenog tima. Katkad i bez kormilara, kako na sljedećim stranicama navodi Šimun Anđelinović. A ipak, uz vjeru i ustrajnost, uz povoljan vjetar i povremeno čvrstu ruku onoga koji zna kojim smjerom treba ići, moguće je učiniti i ono što se u danom trenutku čini nemogućim.

Open Doors

Prof. Željana Bašić, PhD

At the beginning of the Homeland War, I was still a child, yet the war lasted long enough for me to reach my teenage years during it. It would have lasted too long even if it had lasted for a single day, and not more than 1,600 days. Today, more than thirty years later, the number of the missing still exceeds 1,600, and we are still searching for them. I remember scenes from television that remained etched in my memory like haunting vignettes. Who could then have imagined that decades later I would be writing the foreword to the monograph you now hold in your hands?

Some of the people whose stories are told in this book I have met personally, others through the stories of those who knew them, and with some I have, over time, formed friendships. Their stories appear here both directly and indirectly, woven into a broader narrative that reaches beyond individual experiences. I will only briefly highlight what moved me most during the preparation of these materials, leaving it to each reader to recognize what matters most to them.

This monograph does not attempt to encompass everything. If it did, it would contain thousands of scientific, historical, medical, forensic, and deeply human stories. Above all, it reminds us of what a scientific team can achieve. At times, even without a helmsman, as Šimun Anđelinović remarks in the pages that

PDF page 6

Pravo razumijevanje razmjera onoga što se događalo iza bojišnica došlo je tek poslije rata. Tijekom boravka na sveučilištu Penn State, gdje sam davala intervju o Mitchellu M. Hollandu, shvatila sam koliko je truda, ustrajnosti, vjere, znanja i humanosti, ali i tuge i sreće, utkano u identifikaciju žrtava Domovinskoga rata. Novinarki koja ga je poznavala isključivo kao uglađenoga i nenametljivoga sveučilišnog profesora, pokušala sam približiti jednu drugu dimenziju njegove biografije – njegov dolazak u Hrvatsku. Činjenica da je napustio

follow. And yet, with faith and perseverance, with a favorable wind and, at moments, the steady hand of someone who knows the direction, it becomes possible to accomplish what, at a given moment, seems impossible.

A true understanding of the scale of what was happening behind the front lines came only after the war. During my time at Penn State University, where I was being interviewed about Mitchell M. Holland, I began to grasp how much effort, perseverance, faith, knowledge, and humanity, as well as sorrow and moments of happiness, had been woven into the identification of the victims of the Homeland War. To a journalist who knew him only as a refined and unassuming university professor, I tried to reveal another dimension of his life: his arrival in Croatia. The fact that he left behind his family, his home, his work, and the security of life in the United States to take part in the identification of war victims still seems almost unimaginable to me. At the time, he was the director of the Armed Forces DNA Identification Laboratory (AFDIL) in Rockville, Maryland, a position from which one rarely steps into the unknown. And yet, he came to Croatia. He did so at the invitation of his friend and colleague Dragan Primorac, who had himself trained at AFDIL, driven by a deep belief that science must serve the truth and that it knows no boundaries.

The Croatian model, as you will soon see, rose like a phoenix from the ashes of war. For this to happen, many conditions had to align simul-

obitelj, dom, posao i sigurnost američkoga života kako bi sudjelovao u identifikaciji žrtava rata i danas mi se čini gotovo nevjerojatnom. U to je vrijeme bio direktor laboratorija Armed Forces DNA Identification Laboratory (AFDIL) u Rockvilleu, u Marylandu, na poziciji koju se rijetko napušta radi odlaska u nepoznato. A ipak je došao u Hrvatsku. To je napravio na poziv prijatelja i kolege Dragana Primorca, koji je i sam prošao edukaciju u AFDIL-u jer je duboko vjerovao da znanost mora služiti istini i da ne poznaje granice.

Hrvatski je model, kao što ćete uskoro vidjeti, izrastao poput feniksa iz pepela rata. Da bi se to dogodilo, morale su se istodobno ostvariti mnoge okolnosti na različitim mjestima, na različitim razinama i u različitim dijelovima svijeta. Sve je započelo ratom, ali nije njime završilo. Nastavlja se i danas u potrazi za nestalima koje još uvijek tražimo, u znanstvenoj suradnji koja traje i u novim otkrićima koja nadograđuju tada postavljene temelje.

Paralelno s tim procesima razvijala se i inicijativa Matka Marušića, koji je prepoznao jednu važnu činjenicu. U Sjedinjenim Američkim Državama liječnici i doktori znanosti bili su u ravnopravnome položaju. Upravo mu je to

Čekaonica Kliničkoga bolničkog centra Split tijekom Domovinskoga rata (foto: Phil Farnsworth, uz dopuštenje).

Waiting room of the University Hospital of Split during the Homeland War (photo: Phil Farnsworth, used with permission).

taneously across different places, levels, and parts of the world. Everything began with the war, but it did not end there. It continues even today, in the search for those who are still missing, in ongoing scientific collaboration, and in new discoveries that build upon the foundations laid in those years.

In parallel with these processes, the initiative of Matko Marušić was also taking shape, as he recognized an important fact. In the United States, physicians and scientists with doctoral degrees are on equal footing. This is precisely what enabled him to send his graduate medical students abroad for postdoctoral training. Over the years, he sent around 150 of them, relying on what he himself called a “coefficient of utilization.” He believed that those who succeeded would bring knowledge, experience, and development to Croatia. Among them was Dragan Primorac, whose departure was also supported by Šimun Anđelinović, then (and now) head of the Clinical Department of Pathology, Forensic Medicine, and Cytology at the University Hospital of Split. The friendship between Anđelinović and Primorac went beyond science itself, as they were also connected through the taekwondo club Kocunar, founded by Primorac. On 14 January 1991, Primorac also included the club’s first senior team in the defense of Croatia through the Ministry of the Interior. The further course of his life and career was deeply marked by the loss of six of his friends and students, whom he still mentions with pride today. This was also a strong motivation for him to succeed in the identification project that would soon follow.

PDF page 8

omogućilo da svoje diplomirane studente medicine upućuje na poslijedoktorsko usavršavanje u inozemstvo. Tijekom godina poslao ih je oko 150, oslanjajući se na vlastiti, kako ga je nazivao, „koeficijent iskoristivosti“. Vjerovao je da će oni koji tamo uspiju donijeti znanje, iskustvo i razvoj. Među njima je bio i Dragan Primorac, čiji je odlazak zagovarao i Šimun Anđelinović, tada (i sada) predstojnik Kliničkoga zavoda za patologiju, sudsku medicinu i citologiju Kliničkoga bolničkog centra Split. Prijateljstvo Anđelinovića i Primorca nadilazilo je samu znanost jer su bili povezani i taekwondo klubom Kocunar, koji je osnovao Primorac. Primorac je, također, 14. siječnja 1991. godine prvu seniorsku ekipu uključio u obranu Hrvatske, u okviru Ministarstva unutarnjih poslova. Njegov daljnji život i karijeru duboko je obilježio gubitak šestorice prijatelja i učenika, koje i dandanas s ponosom spominje. To mu je bio i veliki motiv da uspiju u projektu identifikacije koji će uskoro uslijediti.

Početkom 1993. godine, tijekom boravka na Klinici za pedijatriju u Farmingtonu u sklopu ustanove University of Connecticut Health Center, Dragan Primorac primio je poziv upravo od Šimuna Anđelinovića. Razgovor koji je uslijedio bio je usmjeren na jedno ključno pitanje: kako pronaći rješenje za identifikaciju velikoga broja žrtava iz masovnih grobnica koje nije bilo moguće identificirati standardnim forenzičkim metodama? Naizgled, rješenja nije bilo.

Tjedan dana nakon toga razgovora možemo zamisliti kako se, na različitim stranama svijeta i u različitim okruženjima, istodobno odvijaju prizori koji će uskoro postati dijelom iste priče. U čekaonici patologije stoje majke i čekaju vijesti o tome jesu li tijela njihove djece u obdukcijskoj sali s druge strane zida. U toj istoj sali Šimun Anđelinović, Marija Definis i obdukcijski tehničari pregledavaju tijela, nastojeći pronaći što više osobnih pokazatelja koji bi mogli pomoći u identifikaciji. U

At the beginning of 1993, while at the Department of Pediatrics in Farmington at the University of Connecticut Health Center, Dragan Primorac received a call from Šimun Anđelinović. The conversation that followed was focused on one key question: how to find a solution for the identification of a large number of victims from mass graves who could not be identified using standard forensic methods. At first glance, there was no solution.

A week after that conversation, one can imagine how, in different parts of the world and in different settings, scenes were unfolding at the same time that would soon become part of the same story. In the pathology waiting room, mothers stand and wait for news of whether the bodies of their children are in the autopsy room on the other side of the wall. In that same room, Šimun Anđelinović, Marija Definis, and the autopsy technicians examine the bodies, trying to find as many personal indicators as possible that could help the identification. At the same time, Dragan Primorac arrives at Henry Lee’s office in Meriden, Connecticut, wondering how best to make the most of the ten minutes he had been promised. At that moment, Henry Lee is probably working on one of his demanding cases, as he was already an American scientific and professional star. Since 1975, he had been the chief criminalist for the state of Connecticut; and the Connecticut State Police forensic laboratory had undergone a thorough scientific transformation under his leadership. In the years that followed, he turned that laboratory into one of the leading forensic centers in the United States. We can only imagine what a moment that was for a young physician from a war-affected country, waiting for a meeting with one of the world’s most renowned forensic experts, aware that the outcome of that encounter could determine the future direction of the entire process. What, meanwhile, might Henry Lee have thought when his secretary introduced the young physician from Croatia? Did

PDF page 9

isto vrijeme, u ured Henryja Leeja u Meridenu, u saveznoj državi Connecticut, dolazi Dragan Primorac i razmišlja kako će najbolje iskoristiti svojih deset obećanih minuta sastanka. Henry Lee u tom trenutku vjerojatno radi na nekome od svojih zahtjevnih slučajeva jer je već bio američka znanstvena i forenzička zvijezda. Od 1975. godine bio je glavni forenzički stručnjak savezne države Connecticut, a forenzički laboratorij Državne policije Connecticuta prošao je pod njegovim vodstvom temeljitu znanstvenu transformaciju. U godinama koje su uslijedile pretvorio je taj laboratorij u jedno od vodećih forenzičkih središta u Sjedinjenim Američkim Državama. Možemo tek pretpostaviti kakav je to bio trenutak za mladoga liječnika iz ratom pogođene zemlje koji čeka razgovor s jednim od najpoznatijih svjetskih forenzičara, svjestan da ishod toga susreta može odrediti daljnji smjer cijeloga procesa. Što li je, s druge strane, Henry Lee mogao pomisliti kada je tajnica uvela mladoga liječnika iz Hrvatske? Je li znao gdje je Hrvatska? Je li znao da je u Hrvatskoj rat? Poslije mi je rekao da ga je odmah zaintrigirala pridošličina visina, što i nije neobično kada se zna da je populacija područja Dalmacije među najvišima u Europi. Razgovor koji je trebao trajati deset minuta potrajao je više od dva i pol sata.

Čini se da su i Henry Lee i Dragan Primorac bili dovoljno znatiželjni i odlučni da prepoznaju kako je riječ o nečemu što će izrasti u veliki zajednički projekt i životno prijateljstvo. Istodobno, Lee je radio na slučaju Helle Crafts, poznatom kao Wood Chipper Case, koji je bitno promijenio dotadašnje shvaćanje forenzičkih identifikacija. Bio je to prvi slučaj u saveznoj državi Connecticut u kojemu su forenzičari imali ključnu ulogu u donošenju osuđujuće presude za ubojstvo bez pronalaska tijela, pri čemu su dokazi u presudnoj mjeri počivali na analizi DNK-a, i to iz koštanih fragmenata i ljudskoga tkiva. U tome je kontekstu Dragan Primorac predložio hitno prikupljanje uzoraka krvi i

he know where Croatia was? Did he know that there was a war in Croatia? Later, he told me that he was immediately intrigued by the visitor’s height, which is not surprising given that the population of the Dalmatian region is among the tallest in Europe. A conversation that was supposed to last ten minutes extended to more than two and a half hours.

It seems that both Henry Lee and Dragan Primorac had enough curiosity and determination to recognize that this would grow into a major joint project and a lifelong friendship. At the same time, Lee was working on the Helle Crafts case, known as the Wood Chipper case, which significantly changed the prevailing understanding of forensic identification. It was the first case in the state of Connecticut in which forensic experts played a key role in securing a murder conviction without the recovery of a body, with the evidence relying to a decisive extent on DNA analysis, including that obtained from bone fragments and human tissue. In this context, Dragan Primorac proposed the urgent collection of blood samples and the establishment of a DNA database of relatives of the missing and the killed in Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. This opened the possibility of a major undertaking, and with that idea, the first glimmer of hope for the families of the missing began to arrive in Croatia. The laboratory, located in the basement of the Connecticut State Police forensic laboratory building, became a place of daily meetings between Lee and Primorac. After their regular duties, they would return to the laboratory and extract DNA from degraded samples, then amplify it using the polymerase chain reaction.

At that moment, the humanitarian organization AmeriCares also entered the story, and the project ceased to be merely scientific. The organization was led by Stephen Skakel, connected to the Kennedy family, and his meeting with Dragan Primorac that followed was not

PDF page 10

uspostavu DNK baze podataka rodbine nestalih i ubijenih u Hrvatskoj i Bosni i Hercegovini. Time se otvorila mogućnost velikoga pothvata, a s tom je idejom u Hrvatsku počelo pristizati i prvo zrno nade za obitelji nestalih. Laboratorij, smješten u podrumu zgrade forenzičkoga laboratorija državne policije Connecticuta, postao je mjesto svakodnevnih susreta Leeja i Primorca. Nakon redovitih obveza vraćali su se u laboratorij i radili na izolaciji DNK-a iz degradiranih uzoraka, koji su potom umnažali lančanom reakcijom polimeraze.

U priču se u tom trenutku uključuje i humanitarna organizacija AmeriCares, čime projekt prestaje biti samo znanstveni. Organizaciju je vodio Stephen Skakel, povezan s obitelji Kennedy, a njegov susret s Draganom Primorcem koji je uslijedio nije bio plod slučajnosti, nego rezultat onoga što će se u ovoj priči uvijek iznova pokazivati presudnim, a to je pomoć prijatelja. Upravo je Stanimir Vuk-Pavlović, istaknuti hrvatsko-američki znanstvenik iz bolnice Mayo Clinic, povezao Dragana Primorca s organizacijom AmeriCares. Njegovo je posredovanje bilo ključno, a putem organizacije Ameri- Cares Primorac je upoznao Barbaru Bush, koja je u toj organizaciji imala važnu stratešku ulogu. Tako je organiziran sastanak s Gaynorom N. Kelleyjem, jednim od najutjecajnijih ljudi američkoga poslovnog svijeta i tadašnjim predsjednikom uprave tvrtke Perkin-Elmer, svjetskoga predvodnika u razvoju analize DNK-a i tehnologije PCR-a, na kojemu su s njim bili Dragan Primorac, Stephen Skakel i Zelda Jacobs. Rezultat je bio prihvaćanje hrvatskoga koncepta sustavne identifikacije žrtava iz masovnih grobnica primjenom DNK tehnologije, u to vrijeme još uvijek u svojim početcima. Primorac je bio uvjeren da će Anđelinović svojim ugledom, energijom i golemim organizacijskim sposobnostima stvoriti sve preduvjete kako bi se u Splitu provelo sve potrebno za DNK identifikaciju žrtava.

a coincidence but rather the result of what would repeatedly prove decisive in this story: the help of friends. It was Stanimir Vuk Pavlović, a distinguished Croatian American scientist from the Mayo Clinic, who connected Dragan Primorac with the organization Ameri- Cares. His mediation was crucial, and through AmeriCares, Primorac met Barbara Bush, who had an important strategic role in the organization. A meeting was thus arranged with Gaynor N. Kelley, one of the most influential figures in the American business world and, at the time, president of Perkin-Elmer, a global leader in the development of DNA analysis and PCR technology, at which Dragan Primorac, Stephen Skakel, and Zelda Jacobs were also present. The result was the acceptance of the Croatian concept of systematic identification of victims from mass graves using DNA technology, which at that time was still in its early stages. Primorac was convinced that Anđelinović, with his reputation, energy, and exceptional organizational abilities, would create all the necessary conditions for the DNA identification of victims to be carried out in Split.

In March 1994, a C-130 Hercules transport aircraft carrying Dragan Primorac, Michael Baden, Mitchell Kennedy, and Stephen Skakel landed in Split, where they were received by Šimun Anđelinović, who immediately took them to the University Hospital of Split. The international team, together with Anđelinović, was joined by Croatian experts Marija Definis, Jakša Ivanović, Irena Drmić, and the laboratory technician Boja Režić. Representatives of Perkin-Elmer believed that several months would be required to establish the laboratory, but the Croatian team accomplished it in a matter of days. The purpose of the visit was clear: focused on DNA analysis of skeletal remains recovered from the Neretva River, which were believed to belong to a boy who had died by drowning almost ten years earlier. By the next morning, the boy’s identity was confirmed, and

PDF page 11

U ožujku 1994. godine transportni zrakoplov C-130 Hercules, s Draganom Primorcem, Michaelom Badenom, Mitchellom Kennedyjem i Stephenom Skakelom, sletio je u Split, gdje ih je dočekao Šimun Anđelinović i odmah ih poveo u Klinički bolnički centar Split. Međunarodnomu timu, uz Anđelinovića, pridružili su se i hrvatski stručnjaci: Marija Definis, Jakša Ivanović, Irena Drmić te laboratorijska tehničarka Boja Režić. Predstavnici Perkin-Elmera smatrali su da će za uspostavu laboratorija biti potrebno nekoliko mjeseci, no hrvatski je tim to uspio učiniti u nekoliko dana. Cilj posjeta bio je jasan: bio je posvećen analizi DNK-a koštanih ostataka izvađenih iz rijeke Neretve, za koje se sumnjalo da pripadaju dječaku stradalom u utapanju gotovo deset godina ranije. Već sljedećega jutra potvrđen je dječakov identitet, čime su otvorena vrata identifikaciji žrtava iz masovnih grobnica primjenom DNK tehnologije. Istoga dana, 16. ožujka 1994. godine, laboratorij je i službeno otvoren. Bio je to trenutak u kojemu je Hrvatska zakoračila u svijet suvremene analize DNK-a.

U međuvremenu je tim splitskih forenzičara, koji su činili Šimun Anđelinović, Marija Definis i Dragan Primorac, odlukom Vojnoga suda u Zagrebu i vojnoga istražnog suca Danka Kovača od 18. travnja 1994. godine, određen za provedbu vještačenja uzoraka tkiva izuzetih tijekom očevida na prostoru ostataka skladišta Hrvatske vojske u Dubokome jarku kraj Sesveta. Bio je to prvi slučaj u hrvatskome pravosuđu od osamostaljenja Republike Hrvatske u kojemu je službeno zatražena identifikacija analizom DNK-a. Ona je krajem te godine uspješno završena te su ostatci žrtava vraćeni obiteljima.

U trenutku kada su na Odsjek za patologiju KBC-a Split zaprimljena tijela 61 žrtve iz Kupresa, napori hrvatskih i američkih forenzičara dodatno su se intenzivirali. Klasičnim identifikacijskim metodama identificirano je 35 osoba, dok su preostale ostale nepoznate. Ni

with that, the door was opened to the identification of victims from mass graves using DNA technology. On that same day, 16 March 1994, the laboratory was officially opened. It was a moment when Croatia entered the world of modern DNA analysis.

In the meantime, the Split forensic team, Šimun Anđelinović, Marija Definis, and Dragan Primorac, by decision of the Military Court in Zagreb and the military investigating judge Danko Kovač, dated 18 April 1994, was appointed to carry out expert analysis of tissue samples collected during the investigation at the site of the destroyed Croatian Army storage facility in Duboki Jarak near Sesvete. This was the first case in the Croatian legal system since the country’s independence in which DNA analysis was officially requested for identification. It was successfully completed by the end of that year, and the remains of the victims were returned to their families.

At the moment when the Department of Pathology at the University Hospital of Split received the bodies of 61 civilian victims from Kupres, the efforts of Croatian and American forensic experts intensified further. Using classical identification methods, 35 individuals were identified, while the remaining ones remained unidentified. The team members themselves were not spared of personal tragedies. Dragan Primorac, only a few days after the liberation of the Kupres plateau, was confronted with the death of his relative, Croatian Army brigadier Ante Primorac.

After the liberation of Kupres in November 1994, a team of American forensic experts, including Dragan Primorac, came to Croatia. The team included Henry C. Lee, Barbara Wolf, a forensic pathologist from Albany, New York with experience in medicolegal investigations and expert testimony, Moses Schanfield, a molecular biologist and one of the leading experts in

sami članovi tima nisu bili pošteđeni osobnih tragedija. Tako se Dragan Primorac, samo nekoliko dana nakon oslobađanja Kupreške visoravni, suočio s pogibijom svoga rođaka, brigadira HV-a Ante Primorca.

Nakon oslobađanja Kupresa, u studenome 1994. godine, tim američkih forenzičara s Draganom Primorcem dolazi u Hrvatsku. U sastavu tima bili su Henry C. Lee, Barbara Wolf, forenzička patologinja iz Albanyja (New York) s iskustvom u sudskomedicinskim istragama i sudskim vještačenjima, Moses Schanfield, molekularni biolog i jedan od vodećih stručnjaka za forenzičku genetiku i analizu DNK-a iz Denvera, te Michael Baden, istaknuti forenzički patolog i tadašnji suvoditelj jedinice Forensic Sciences Unit Državne policije New Yorka, poznat po radu na složenim slučajevima nasilnih smrti i masovnih stradanja. S hrvatskim forenzičarima, predvođenim Anđelinovićem, te uz aktivno sudjelovanje članova obitelji nestalih, započeli su sustavnu i metodološki strogu analizu koštanih ostataka i osobnih predmeta pronađenih u kupreškim masovnim grobnicama. Među tim predmetima nalazili su se odjevni predmeti, križići, privjesci, upaljači i drugi tragovi. Ubrzo potom započela je i sustavna analiza DNK-a, proces koji je trajno promijenio način identifikacije žrtava masovnih stradanja.

Američki forenzičari koji su došli u Hrvatsku i Bosnu i Hercegovinu nisu provodili vrijeme samo u laboratoriju. Susreli su se s iskustvima kakva do tada, pa ni u najsloženijim slučajevima, nisu imali prilike vidjeti. U ekshumacijama masovnih grobnica u Petrinji i na Kupresu suočeni su s uvjetima koji su nadilazili uobičajene okvire forenzičkoga rada. Lee je uvijek bio iznimno metodičan, čemu sam svjedočila godinama poslije, vidjevši ga kako se priprema za izlazak na teren. Prije izlaska na mjesto događaja na otvorenome prostoru, nanosio bi zaštitnu kremu za sunce i nudio je kolegama, svjestan da se nikad ne zna koliko će

forensic genetics and DNA analysis from Denver, and Michael Baden, a prominent forensic pathologist and at the time co-head of the Forensic Sciences Unit of the New York State Police, known for his work on complex cases of violent death and mass fatalities. Together with Croatian forensic experts led by Anđelinović, and with the active participation of family members of the missing, they began a systematic and methodologically rigorous analysis of skeletal remains and personal items recovered from the mass graves in Kupres. Among these items were clothing, crosses, pendants, lighters, and other evidence. Soon afterward, systematic DNA analysis began, a process that permanently changed the way victims of mass fatalities are identified.

The American forensic experts who came to Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina did not spend their time only in the laboratory. They were confronted with scenes unlike anything they had seen before, even in the most complex cases. During the exhumations of mass graves in Kupres and Petrinja, they faced conditions that went beyond the usual environment of forensic work. Lee was always extremely methodical, as I witnessed years later when I saw how he prepared for field work. Before heading to an outdoor scene, he would apply sunscreen and offer it to colleagues, aware that one never knows how long fieldwork might last. Yet, no matter how prepared he was for uncertainty, he could not have been prepared for what awaited them. In the mass graves in Kupres, explosive devices had been placed on and around the bodies of the victims. One wrong step could have been fatal for everyone present and could have destroyed the already fragile traces of identity. Only a few days before their arrival, a military policeman informed them that a dog that had stepped off the path they were following had triggered a landmine. Despite this, Henry Lee joined the teams in the field, maintaining his characteristic spirit of solidarity and pro-

PDF page 14

dugo trajati rad na terenu. No, koliko god bio spreman na neizvjesnost, nije mogao biti pripremljen na ono što ih je dočekalo. U masovnim grobnicama na Kupresu na tijelima i uz tijela žrtava bile su postavljene minske i eksplozivne naprave. Jedan pogrješan pokret mogao je biti koban za sve prisutne, a ujedno i uništiti, ionako krhke, tragove identiteta. Samo nekoliko dana prije njihova dolaska, vojni policajac ih je obavijestio da je pas koji je izašao s puta kojim su se kretali aktivirao nagaznu minu. Unatoč tomu, Henry Lee ušao je među timove na terenu, zadržavajući svoj prepoznatljivi duh zajedništva i profesionalne odgovornosti. Kad ga je u jednom trenutku Primorac upitao želi li, zbog sigurnosti, odustati od daljnjega rada, bez razmišljanja je odgovorio da su zajedno došli i da će zajedno otići te da im je prioritet utvrditi istinu. U susretima s obiteljima znao je reći da su i oni u tom trenutku njegova obitelj, čime je jasno pokazivao da njegov angažman nadilazi stručni okvir. S druge strane, kako je isticao Šimun Anđelinović, lokalno stanovništvo i obitelji doživljavali su članove međunarodnoga tima gotovo kao spasitelje, kao posljednju nadu da će pronaći i identificirati svoje najmilije. U tom međusobnom prepoznavanju, između onih koji traže i onih koji pomažu, oblikovao se prostor povjerenja bez kojega takav proces ne bi bio moguć.

Izloženi osobni predmeti korišteni u identifikaciji žrtava u obdukcijskoj sali Kliničkoga bolničkog centra Split (izvor: arhiv Kliničkoga zavoda za patologiju, sudsku medicinu i citologiju KBC-a Split).

Personal belongings used in the identification of victims displayed in the autopsy room of the University Hospital of Split (source: Archive of the Clinical Department of Pathology, Forensic Medicine, and Cytology, University Hospital of Split).

fessional responsibility. When Primorac asked him, at one point, whether, for safety reasons, he wished to continue, he replied without hesitation that they had come together and would leave together, and that their priority was to establish the truth. In encounters with families, he would say that they, too, were his family at that moment, clearly showing that his engagement went beyond the professional settings. On the other hand, as Šimun Anđelinović emphasized, the local population and the families perceived the members of the international team almost as saviors, as the last hope of finding and identifying their loved ones. In this mutual recognition between those searching for missing family members and those helping them, a space of trust was formed without which such a process would not have been possible.

How important it was that international experts participated in these exhumations is also confirmed by Barbara Wolf’s testimony before the United States Congress on 6 December 1995. In that testimony, given under oath, she directly described the work of the Split team led by Anđelinović, with whom the American experts worked side by side in the autopsy room. As she stated, the international team did not come to take over the process, but to support an already established system: “We came to

PDF page 15

Koliko je bilo važno da su na tim ekshumacijama sudjelovali i međunarodni stručnjaci, potvrđuje i svjedočenje Barbare Wolf pred Kongresom Sjedinjenih Američkih Država, održano 6. prosinca 1995. godine. U tome je svjedočenju, iznesenom pod prisegom, izravno opisala rad splitskoga tima predvođenog Anđelinovićem, s kojim su američki stručnjaci radili rame uz rame u obdukcijskoj sali. Kako je navela, međunarodni tim nije dolazio preuzeti proces, nego pružiti potporu već uspostavljenomu sustavu: „Došli smo u Hrvatsku i Bosnu kako bismo surađivali s forenzičkim timom Kliničkoga bolničkog centra u Splitu i pružili mu podršku.“ Opisujući konkretan rad u Splitu, istaknula je da su američki stručnjaci radili sa Šimunom Anđelinovićem u obdukcijskoj sali, gdje je istodobno bilo izloženo više tijela, a obitelji su ulazile pokušavajući prepoznati svoje bližnje. U takvim uvjetima, već tijekom jednoga dana identificiran je velik broj žrtava, dok se analiza DNK-a nastavila za neidentificirane. Na izravno pitanje o tome jesu li hrvatski stručnjaci kompetentni, odgovorila je bez zadrške: „U potpunosti jesu kompetentni, i mislim da je ovo doista primjer kako znanost nadilazi političke podjele. Tehnike koje se rabe u Sjedinjenim Američkim Državama gotovo su identične onima koje su se rabile ondje, uključujući i DNK laboratorij u Splitu.“ Koliki je značaj Šimuna Anđelinovića bio u tom procesu ne može se svesti isključivo na organizaciju rada i primjenu forenzičkih metoda. Njegova se uloga protezala i na završnu fazu identifikacijskoga postupka, u kojoj se znanstveni nalaz treba pretočiti u razgovor s obiteljima. Upravo u tom prijelazu, iz analitičkoga u ljudski kontekst, ostvarivao se ključni cilj cijeloga procesa: povratak identiteta i dostojanstva. Susreti s obiteljima, često obilježeni snažnim emocionalnim reakcijama i neposrednim fizičkim kontaktom, svjedoče o tome da identifikacija nije bila samo tehnički čin, nego i završni korak u procesu razrješenja dugotrajne neizvjesnosti.

Croatia and Bosnia to work with the forensic team of the University Hospital in Split and to provide support.” Describing the work in Split, she emphasized that American experts worked with Šimun Anđelinović in the autopsy room, where several bodies were laid out at the same time, while families entered trying to recognize their relatives. Under such conditions, a large number of victims were identified in a single day, while DNA analysis continued for those who remained unidentified. When directly asked if the Croatian experts were competent, she answered without hesitation: “They absolutely are, and I think this truly is an example of how science transcends political divisions. The techniques used in the United States are largely the same as those used there, including the DNA laboratory in Split.” The importance of Šimun Anđelinović in this process cannot be reduced only to the organization of work and the application of forensic methods. His role extended to the final stage of the identification process, in which scientific findings are translated into communication with families. It is precisely in this transition, from the analytical to the human context, that the key goal of the entire process was achieved: the restoration of identity and dignity. Encounters with families, often marked by strong emotional reactions and direct physical contact, testify that identification was not only a technical act, but also the final step in resolving prolonged uncertainty.

In this story, Professor Matko Marušić, then editor of the Croatian Medical Journal, was constantly present in the background, insisting that all aspects of the experience of forensic identification of victims be appropriately published in scientific form. Even today, as I write this foreword, it is clear to me how right he was. As much as possible was published, perhaps too much for those who spent their days in the autopsy room, and never enough for Marušić. Publication activity in-

PDF page 16

U priču je stalno bio nevidljivo uključen i profesor Matko Marušić, tada urednik časopisa Croatian Medical Journal, koji je ustrajao na tome da se svi aspekti iskustva forenzičke identifikacije žrtava primjereno znanstveno publiciraju. Koliko je bio u pravu, vidim i danas tijekom pisanja ovog predgovora. Koliko se moglo, publiciralo se; onima koji su dane provodili u obdukcijskoj sali i previše, a Marušiću uvijek premalo. Publiciranje je ubrzano krajem 1994. godine, kada je Marušić spojio Primorca i urednika ključnoga američkog časopisa JAMA, Phila Gunbyja s kojim je prijateljevao i JAMA je objavila članak o suradnji hrvatskih i američkih stručnjaka na DNK identifikaciji žrtava rata u

tensified at the end of 1994, when Marušić connected Primorac with Phil Gunby, a friend and editor at the leading American medical journal JAMA. The journal subsequently published an article on the collaboration between Croatian and American experts in the DNA identification of war victims in Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina. Since JAMA is an extremely influential journal, invitations soon followed to present the Croatian-American collaboration in other journals and at scientific meetings. Already in December 1994, at Lee’s invitation, Primorac presented the Croatian model of identification of victims from mass graves using DNA technology at an international conference in Taipei (Taiwan), which gathered leading forensic experts of that time. In this way, the global scientific community was introduced to the approach developed under wartime conditions in Croatia for the first time. Additional international visibility was provided by an article published in 1995 in Science, which highlighted the scientific and humanitarian significance of identifying missing persons in Croatia.

In February 1996, Primorac and Anđelinović participated in the 48th meeting of the American Academy of Forensic Sciences in Nashville, with more than 2,500 participants. They presented the results of the identification of victims from mass graves in Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina, further confirming the Croatian experience within the international professional community.

Article “Split Pathologists in the American Academy,” Slobodna Dalmacija, 7 March 1996.

Hrvatskoj i Bosni i Hercegovini. Budući da je JAMA iznimno utjecajan časopis, slijedili su pozivi da se hrvatsko-američka priča predstavi i u drugim časopisima i znanstvenim skupovima. Već u prosincu 1994. godine, na poziv Leeja, Primorac je predstavio „hrvatski model“ identifikacije žrtava masovnih grobnica primjenom DNK tehnologije na međunarodnoj konferenciji u Taipeiju (Tajvan), koja je okupila tada vodeće forenzičare svijeta. Time je svjetska znanstvena zajednica prvi put bila izravno upoznata s pristupom razvijenim u ratnim uvjetima u Hrvatskoj. Dodatnu međunarodnu vidljivost donio je i članak objavljen 1995. godine u časopisu Science, koji je istaknuo znanstveni i humanitarni značaj identifikacije nestalih osoba u Hrvatskoj.

U veljači 1996. godine, Primorac i Anđelinović sudjelovali su na 48. konferenciji Američke akademije za forenzičke znanosti u Nashvilleu, koji je okupio više od dvije i pol tisuće sudionika. Iznijeli su rezultate identifikacije žrtava iz masovnih grobnica u Hrvatskoj i Bosni i Hercegovini, čime je hrvatsko iskustvo dodatno potvrđeno u međunarodnome stručnom prostoru.

U travnju 1996. godine u časopisu Journal of Forensic Sciences objavljen je znanstveni rad koji je dokumentirao rezultate identifikacije kupreških žrtava analizom DNK-a. Danas se ta publikacija smatra prvom recenziranom studijom u svijetu koja je sustavno prikazala identifikaciju velikoga broja žrtava masovnih grobnica tom tehnologijom, čime su postavljeni temelji suvremene forenzičke genetike u kontekstu ratnih i masovnih zločina.

Daljnji razvoj i prijenos znanja potvrđeni su i 1997. godine kad se održao European-American Intensive Course in PCR-Based Clinical and Forensic Testing u Splitu, preteča kasnijih ISABS kongresa, uz ključnu potporu tvrtke Perkin-Elmer. Time je Hrvatska, iz prostora ratne improvizacije, postala mjesto međunarodne znanstvene razmjene i edukacije.

In April 1996, a paper was published in the Journal of Forensic Sciences documenting the results of the identification of victims from Kupres using DNA technology. Today, this publication is considered the first peer-reviewed study in the world to systematically present the identification of a large number of victims from mass graves using this technology, thereby laying the foundations of modern forensic genetics in the context of war and mass crimes.

In 1997, the European-American Intensive Course in PCR-Based Clinical and Forensic

PDF page 18

Iza toga razvoja nije stajao samo sustav, nego i ljudi koji su ga nosili. U tom se procesu povezivalo više razina i prostora. Amerika je bila polazište, Primorac ključna poveznica prema Hrvatskoj i znanstvenik koji je osigurao prijenos stručnoga znanja u Hrvatsku, dok je u Splitu Anđelinović preuzimao odgovornost za njegovu primjenu u praksi. Njegov rad nije bio samo medicinski i forenzički, nego duboko ljudski. Svakodnevno je stajao između znanosti i obitelji, između tijela bez imena i ljudi koji su tražili svoje najbliže. U tome prostoru, gdje su se susretali bol, nada i istina, oblikovao se način rada koji će uskoro postati prepoznat kao hrvatski model. No, još važnije od toga, ondje se svakodnevno događalo ono što je Anđelinović i sam isticao kao bit svega, a to je vraćanje imena i dostojanstva onima kojima su oni u smrti bili oduzeti.

Zato naslovnica ove knjige ne prikazuje ljude. Prikazuje vrata. Otvorena vrata bolničkoga prostora, gledana iz hodnika, bez ijedne osobe u kadru. Ipak, ona nisu prazna, nego su ispunjena prisutnošću svih koji su kroz njih prolazili ili pred njima čekali. S jedne strane tih vrata stajale su majke, očevi i obitelji žrtava, između nade i istine, čekajući odgovor na pitanje koje određuje život. S druge strane bili su znanstvenici, liječnici i tehničari, jednako tako u iščekivanju, tražili su način da iz tišine smrti izvuku pokojnikovo ime. Ta vrata nisu bila samo ulaz u obdukcijsku salu ili čekaonicu. Bila su granica između nepoznatoga i poznatoga. Između broja i imena. Između nestanka i pronalaska. Ali bila su i početak. Jer upravo su se kroz ta vrata otvorili putovi prema nečemu novome, prema tehnologiji koja je tek tada nastajala, prema znanosti koja je tražila svoje granice, prema suradnji koja je povezala kontinente, prema prijateljstvima koja su nadživjela rat. Kroz njih se počeo oblikovati hrvatski model, nastao u najtežim uvjetima i prepoznat u svijetu. Kroz njih su dolazili oni koji su pomagali, i odlazili oni koji su na-

Testing was organized in Split with key support from Perkin-Elmer. The course later became a precursor to the ISABS conferences. In this way, Croatia, from a place of wartime improvisation, became a center of international scientific exchange and education.

Behind this development stood not only a system but also the people who carried it out. In this process, multiple levels and spaces were connected. America was the starting point, Primorac the key link to Croatia, and the scientist who ensured the transfer of know-how, while in Split, Anđelinović assumed responsibility for its practical application. His work was not only medical and forensic, but deeply human. Every day, he stood between science and families, between bodies without names and people searching for their loved ones. In that space, where pain, hope, and truth met, a way of working was formed that would soon become recognized as the Croatian model. Yet even more important than that, what took place there each day was what Anđelinović himself emphasized as the essence of everything: the restoration of names and dignity to those from whom they had been taken in death.

For this reason, the cover of this book does not depict people. It shows doors. Open doors of a hospital space, seen from a corridor, with no one in the frame. And yet they are not empty, but filled with the presence of all those who passed through them or waited before them. On one side of those doors stood mothers, fathers, and families of the victims, between hope and truth, waiting for an answer to the question that defines a life. On the other side were scientists, physicians, and technicians, equally waiting, searching for a way to bring a name out of the silence of death. These doors were not only an entrance to an autopsy room or a waiting area. They were a boundary between the unknown and the known. Between a number and a name. Between disappearance and finding. But

pokon pronađeni. Kroz njih se nije prolazilo samo fizički, nego i iz neizvjesnosti u spoznaju, iz šutnje u istinu. I zato na naslovnici nema nikoga. Jer svi su čekali. I oni koji su tražili, i oni koji su pronalazili. I upravo u tom zajedničkom čekanju, između vrata koja su uvijek bila otvorena, nastajalo je ono najvažnije – povratak dostojanstva.

they were also a beginning. Because it was through these doors that paths opened toward something new, toward a technology that was only just emerging, toward a science that was searching for its limits, toward a collaboration that connected continents, toward friendships that outlived the war. Through them, the Croatian model began to take shape under the most difficult conditions and came to be recognized worldwide. Through them came those who helped, and through them departed those who were finally found. Passing through them was not only a physical act, but also a movement from uncertainty to knowledge, from silence to truth. And that is why there is no one on the cover. Because everyone was waiting. Those who searched, and those who found. And it is precisely in that shared waiting, between doors that were always open, that something essential was created: the restoration of dignity.

A Testimony of a Man: A Conversation with Professor Emeritus Šimun Anđelinović

PDF page 22

Povratak dostojanstva: identifikacija nestalih u Domovinskome ratu

Jasenka Leskur Staničić, novinarka Slobodne Dalmacije: intervju s dr. Šimunom Anđelinovićem

Prema podatcima Ministarstva hrvatskih branitelja, na popisu osoba nestalih i nasilno odvedenih u Domovinskome ratu nalazi se 1740 osoba, i to je najbolnija posljedica ratnih zbivanja, nakon više od 30 godina što je rat okončan. Ni nakon toliko godina članovi 1740 obitelji, majke i očevi, djeca, supruge i rođaci nisu završili potragu za posmrtnim ostatcima svojih najmilijih, u želji da ih sahrane i napokon nađu toliko iščekivan mir.

Proces pomaganja da se taj čestiti i humani cilj ostvari počeo je ratne 1993. godine ekshumacijama masovnih grobnica u Hrvatskoj i Bosni i Hercegovini te identifikacijama žrtava, pri čemu se uloga splitskih stručnjaka za patologiju i sudsku medicinu pokazala – pionirskom.

Suočeni s teško raspadnutim i međusobno izmiješanim ljudskim ostatcima u grobnicama, kada su klasične metode identifikacije postale nedovoljne, Splićani su uveli u postupak analize DNK-a – prvi u svijetu. U ratno doba, u ožujku

Bringing Back Dignity: Identification of the Missing in the Homeland War

Jasenka Leskur Staničić, journalist at Slobodna Dalmacija: An Interview with Dr Šimun Anđelinović

Translated by Željana Bašić

According to the data from the Ministry of Croatian Veterans, 1,740 persons are still listed as missing or forcibly taken during the Homeland War in Croatia, representing the most painful consequence of the war, more than 30 years after its end. Even after so many years, members of 1,740 families, mothers and fathers, children, spouses, and relatives, have not ceased searching for the remains of their loved ones, hoping to give them a proper burial and finally attain the long-awaited peace they deserve.

Efforts to achieve this deeply compassionate and honorable goal began in 1993, during the war, with the exhumation of mass graves in Croatia and Bosnia and Herzegovina and the identification of victims. In this process, pathology and forensic experts from Split played a pioneering role.

Faced with extremely decomposed and commingled human remains, for which conventional identification methods had become insufficient, the

PDF page 23

1994. godine, u Kliničkome bolničkom centru Split uspostavljen je prvi sustavni program DNK identifikacije žrtava iz masovnih grobnica. Štoviše, laboratorij je uspostavljen u manje od dva dana, što je bilo ravno čudu jer je očekivani rok podrazumijevao više mjeseci!

U središtu cijeloga procesa bili su dr. Šimun Anđelinović, kao voditelj splitske Patologije, i njegov bivši student, dr. Dragan Primorac, tada na poslijedoktorskome usavršavanju u SAD-u. Primorac je uspostavio kontakte s američkim stručnjacima, među ostalim s Henryjem C. Leejem, vrhunskim policijskim forenzičarem, u čijoj je biografiji upisan i rad na globalno poznatome slučaju O.J. Simpsona.

Suradnja s američkim kolegama dovela je do brzoga prijenosa znanja i tehnologije u Hrvatsku, uključujući uspostavu DNK laboratorija i primjenu metoda PCR-a na degradiranim koštanim uzorcima. Paralelno su prikupljani referentni uzorci – krv i kosa članova obitelji nestalih osoba – čime je stvorena jedna od prvih srodničkih baza podataka u ratnim uvjetima.

Rad splitskoga tima zabilježen je i u američkim medijima; televizijska kuća WTNH (News 8) snimila je 1995. dokumentarac Heroes of Hope, u kojemu su prvi put prikazane ekshumacije, improvizirane obdukcijske prostorije i susreti s obiteljima nestalih u ratu.

Model uspostavljen u Splitu, kombinacija ekshumacije, laboratorijske analize i baze srodničkih podataka, poslije je primijenjen u međunarodnim programima, uključujući rad Međunarodne komisije za nestale osobe, koja je identificirala više od 27 000 osoba na području bivše Jugoslavije.

Sjećanja na ta izazovna, ali vrlo značajna vremena podijelio je s nama dr. Šimun Anđelinović, kojega je rat zatekao na čelu splitske Patologije kao mladog specijalista u tridesetim godinama života.

Split team introduced DNA analysis into the identification process - the first in the world. In March 1994, during the war, the first systematic program for DNA-based identification of mass grave victims was established at the University Hospital of Split. What’s more, the laboratory was set up in less than two days, which was considered extraordinary, given that the expected timeframe was several months!

Central to this process were Dr Šimun Anđelinović, head of the Clinical Department of Pathology, Forensic Medicine, and Cytology, University Hospital of Split, and his former student, Dr Dragan Primorac, then a postdoctoral researcher in the United States, who established contacts with the U.S. experts, including Dr Henry C. Lee, a leading forensic scientist known for his work on the high-profile O.J. Simpson case.

Collaboration with American colleagues enabled the rapid transfer of knowledge and technology to Croatia, including the establishment of a DNA laboratory and the application of PCR methods to degraded bone samples. At the same time, reference samples, blood and hair from relatives of missing persons were collected, leading to the creation of one of the earliest kinship-based databases under wartime conditions.

The work of the Split team was also documented in the U.S. media. In 1995, the television station WTNH (News 8) produced the documentary Heroes of Hope, which, for the first time, presented exhumations, improvised autopsy facilities, and encounters with the families of the missing.

The model developed in Split, combining exhumation, laboratory analysis, and a kinship-based database, was later applied in international programs, including those of the International Commission on Missing Persons, which has iden-

PDF page 24

Anđelinović: Sve se tada događalo velikom brzinom i malo toga se moglo predvidjeti. Ratni sukobi u bolnicu donijeli su ne samo veliki broj ranjenika, nego i mrtvih tijela koja smo mi kao znanstvenici morali medicinski obraditi, ali i napraviti sve da se ona mogu predati roditeljima, njihovim obiteljima. Taj posao može izgledati jednostavan kad je tijelo nepromijenjeno, međutim, kad imate masovne grobnice, iznakažena tijela, onda klasične metode ne rade. Bilo je to vrijeme kada su patologija u Dubrovniku i Mostaru bile izvan funkcije, a Split je ostao jedino mjesto u južnoj Hrvatskoj gdje je postojala organizirana bolnica koja to može prihvatiti, koja može odraditi redovan posao i nove poslove koji se nameću. Nismo mogli predvidjeti u kojem će se obujmu pojaviti Bosna i Hercegovina kao problem, s obzirom na to da sve do Sarajeva, čak i do Save, nije bilo organizirane patologije i sva tijela iz BiH dolazila su k nama, bilo da smo mi išli na teren po njih ili da su ih drugi dovozili u Split. Tada je naš odjel imao nešto malo opreme koja nije bila bogzna što, imali smo dobru obdukcijsku salu, ali i – veliki entuzijazam. Cijeli tim smo, u početku, činili dr. Marija Definis-Gojanović i ja, uz dr. Jakšu Ivanovića koji nije išao na teren, ali nam je bio potpora u jednako važnim poslovima administracije i razgovora s roditeljima umrlih. Dva sudska medicinara su obavljala sve! U kongresnome izvještaju forenzičarke dr. Barbare Wolf stoji njezin komentar da bi posao koji zahtijeva mjesec dana u Splitu odrađivali u dva dana. U jednoj godini smo, ne znam je li 95. ili 96. godina, stručno, sudskomedicinski obradili 945 tijela. Dva patologa! A prosječno se može napraviti 200 pa onda znate koja je to razina napora bila. A u Bosni su sve okolnosti bile potpuno drukčije nego u Hrvatskoj.

Leskur Staničić: Što to zapravo znači?

Povratak dostojanstva: identifikacija nestalih u Domovinskome ratu /

Bringing Back Dignity: Identification of the Missing in the Homeland War

tified more than 27,000 individuals from the territory of the former Yugoslavia.

Dr Šimun Anđelinović, who found himself leading the Department of Pathology in Split as a young specialist in his thirties when the war began, recalls those challenging, yet deeply significant times.

Anđelinović: Things were happening fast back then, and very little could be predicted. The war brought not only a large number of wounded to the hospital, but also the dead, bodies that we, as scientists, had to examine and process, but also do everything we could to return them to their families. That task might seem straightforward when a body is intact. But when you are dealing with mass graves, with disfigured and damaged remains, the usual methods simply no longer work. It was a time when pathology services in Dubrovnik and Mostar were no longer functioning, and Split remained the only place in southern Croatia with an organized hospital capable of taking this on, handling both its regular work and the new demands that kept emerging. We could not foresee the scale at which Bosnia and Herzegovina would emerge as a problem, given that there was no organized pathology service from Sarajevo all the way to the Sava River, and all bodies from Bosnia and Herzegovina were brought to us - either we went out into the field to retrieve them, or others transported them to Split. Back then, our department had very modest equipment—nothing to write home about, really. We did have a decent autopsy room, but what truly kept us going was an enormous amount of enthusiasm. At the beginning, it was basically just Dr Marija Definis-Gojanović and me, with Dr Jakša Ivanović, who didn’t go out into the field but supported us in other equally important ways, handling administration and,

PDF page 25

Anđelinović: U tom je ratu bilo puno međususjedskih ubojstava, pa je tako svaka strana u sukobu željela napraviti sve da se ubijeni nikad ne identificira, da se uništi mrtvo tijelo. To je sve otežavalo. Primjerice, pri iskopavanju jedne jame naišli smo na eksplozivnu napravu zavezanu žicom za nogu mrtvaca. Da se pomaknula, svi bismo odletjeli u zrak. Mrtve su raznosili eksplozivom, spaljivali, tijela su bila u katastrofalnome stanju. U prvome slučaju gdje smo primijenili DNA analizu, u slučaju eksplozije u Dubokom jarku kraj Sesveta, nalazili smo tek dijelove tijela. Jednog čovjeka prepoznali smo po komadu njegove donje čeljusti i malome komadu mišića i rekli njegovu ocu da je to njegov sin. Ničeg drugog nije bilo, to je sve što smo od njega imali. Hrvatsko selo Uzdol u srednjoj Bosni bilo je likvidirano. Tu je bilo žrtava od sedam do 87 godina, otkidanih ušiju poslije smrti, amputiranih... Strašno, cijelo selo! Bilo je teže i drukčije. U Hrvatskoj je rad na identifikacijama bio relativno dobro organiziran, sudjelovali smo u timu koji je odlazio po tjedan dana u Vukovar. Dnevno smo identificirali od deset do 15 tijela. Bilo je bolno i teško, ali višestruko bolje organizirano, sa šatorima za psihološku pomoć rodbini. U BiH je sve bila čista improvizacija u kojoj se vi, kao znanstvenik, snalazite da biste napravili maksimum u zadanim uvjetima, da se slučaj može sudskomedicinski potpisati i tijelo predati rodbini. Nailazite na ogromne probleme, primjerice, da u nekim dijelovima BiH rodbina, zbog ratnih propusnica, može doći samo u određenim periodima na identifikaciju i vraća se kući noću, a nekad ne mogu ni tada. Imate limitirano vrijeme, raseljenu rodbinu koja više nije u Bosni i Hercegovini, nedostatak osobnih podataka i informacija tko je sve uopće bio tu. Bilo je ljudi iz svih dijelova Hrvatske koji su se tamo zatekli i borili. I – puno civila.

talking to the families of the deceased. Two forensic pathologists were doing everything! In her report to Congress, Dr Barbara Wolf even noted that work which would normally take a month was being done in Split in just two days. In one year, I think it was ’95 or maybe ’96, we conducted forensic examinations of 945 bodies. Two pathologists! The average is around 200, so you can imagine the level of effort that went into it. And in Bosnia, the conditions were completely different from those in Croatia.

Leskur Staničić: What does that actually mean?

Anđelinović: In that war, there were many cases of neighbors killing neighbors, and each side in the conflict often wanted to make sure the victims would never be identified, to destroy the body completely. That made everything much harder. For example, during one excavation, we came across an explosive device tied with a wire to the leg of a dead body. If it had moved, we would all have been blown up. Bodies were torn apart with explosives, burned, they were in a terrible condition. In the first case, where we applied DNA analysis, Duboki Jarak (near Sesvete), we mostly found only fragments. We identified one man from a piece of his lower jaw and a small bit of muscle, and we had to tell his father that this was his son. There was nothing else; that was all we had of him. The Croatian village of Uzdol in central Bosnia was wiped out. The victims’ age ranged from seven to 87 years old, ears cut off after death, limbs amputated… it was horrific. An entire village! It was harder, and it was different. In Croatia, the identification process was relatively well organized. We were part of a team that would go to Vukovar for a week at a time. We identified ten to fifteen bodies

PDF page 26

Takva situacija zahtijeva od znanstvenika trostruko veći angažman, uz manje podataka za zatvaranje slučaja. Primarnu bismo obradu napravili u Bosni, ali kako ona nije bila dovoljna, tijela smo nosili u Split.

Leskur Staničić: Po čemu pamtite period ekshumacije grobnica na Kupresu u kojima je sudjelovao američki tim i koji je dokumentiran u njihovim medijima?

Anđelinović: Vlada Herceg Bosne imala je odgovorne ljude koji bi pripremili teren za nas: doznali bi za lokaciju grobnice i tko su tu bile potencijalne žrtve. Uvijek je postojao neki svjedok. I mi bismo došli i počeli iskopavati. U nekim slučajevima oni bi tijela sami dovezli u Split. Imali smo tehničku pomoć pri iskopavanju, tijela smo spremali u vreće, obradili ih i javili rodbini. A rodbina poginulih bi otprilike znala tko je u tim grobnicama, ali nije bila sigurna je li to stvarno, je li doista tako. Na Kupresu smo radili u više navrata, dvije, tri godine. Naši su ljudi imali jednu specifičnost, nitko nije htio pokopati tijelo svoga pokojnika, a da ga prvo ne vidi. Drugo, željeli su pomoći u iskopavanju. Na takvom jednom iskopavanju čovjek koji nam je pomagao u tehničkoj ekipi prepoznao je u masi leševa - brata. I to po satu koji mu je on kupio! To je strašno, ne možete to ni opisati. Prvo njegovo ushićenje da ga je našao, a nakon toga tuga što ga je pronašao mrtvoga. Bilo je tu životnih situacija koje ste kao znanstvenik mogli samo nijemo promatrati. Američki kolege suočili su se s iskustvima koja se ne mogu dobiti čitajući knjige. Neposrednost naših ljudi, otvorenost i toplina sve je ganula. Ti ljudi su Amerikance doživjeli kao spasitelje. Jedna žena se dirljivo obratila dr. Leeju: „Recite svom predsjedniku da je nama teško…” mislila je da je on došao direktno po njegovu nalogu. Gledali su u njih kao u svetce, to je bilo ostvarenje

Povratak dostojanstva: identifikacija nestalih u Domovinskome ratu /

Bringing Back Dignity: Identification of the Missing in the Homeland War

a day. It was painful and difficult, but far better organized, with tents for psychological support for families, and so on. In Bosnia and Herzegovina, everything was pure improvisation. As a scientist, you had to find a way to do the best you could with what you had, complete the forensic work properly, and return the body to the family. You ran into enormous challenges. In some areas, families could come only for identification at certain times, sometimes traveling back at night, and sometimes not even then, due to wartime permits. Time was limited. Families were displaced, often no longer even in Bosnia and Herzegovina. There was a lack of personal data, a lack of information about who had even been there. People from all over Croatia had ended up there, fighting. And many civilians. In that kind of situation, a scientist has to put in three times more effort, with far less information, to actually close a case. We would do the initial processing in Bosnia, but since it wasn’t sufficient, the bodies were then transported to Split.

Leskur Staničić: What do you remember most about the period of the exhumations in Kupres, in which the U.S. team took part, and which were documented in their media?

Anđelinović: The authorities of Herceg-Bosna had people in charge of preparing the ground for us. They would locate the graves and try to find out who might have been killed there. There was always some kind of witness. And then we would arrive and start the excavation. In some cases, they would even transport the bodies to Split themselves. We had technical support during the excavations. The bodies were placed in bags, examined, and then we would inform the families. The relatives of the victims usually had an idea of who might be in those graves, but they were never certain. They didn’t know for sure if it was really them.

PDF page 27

Novinski članak „Brata sam po satu prepoznao”, Slobodna Dalmacija, o identifikaciji žrtava iz kupreških masovnih grobnica.

Newspaper article “I Recognized My Brother by His Watch,” published in Slobodna Dalmacija, concerning the identification of victims from the Kupres mass graves.

njihovih najvećih želja, zadnja mogućnost da se nešto napravi, da pronađu svoje. Familije su im se povjeravale, mi smo im prevodili. Povjeravali im se kao svojim najbližima, nije bilo barijere između njih.

Leskur Staničić: Kako je izgledao proces identifikacije u Splitu?

Anđelinović: U obdukcijskoj sali, na stolovima, uz svako unakaženo tijelo bismo posložili predmete po kojima bi se eventualno mogli prepoznati, a koje smo našli uz tijela. I onda bi članovi obitelji razgledavali te predmete. Rodbina je u nekim slučajevima prepoznavala svoga po džemperu koji mu je isplela mama. Po satu, krunici, čarapama. U jednom slučaju u pratnji rodbine došla je stomatologinja koja je identificirala tijelo jer je zapamtila što je radila na zubu pokojnika neposredno prije početka rata.

We worked in Kupres on several occasions, over a period of two or three years. There was something very specific about the people there. No one wanted to bury their loved one without seeing the body first. And they wanted to help with the excavations as well. During one such excavation, a man who was helping us as part of the technical team suddenly recognized his brother among the bodies. He knew him by a watch he had once bought for him! It was something you simply cannot describe. First, the surge of relief that he had found him, and then the overwhelming grief of realizing he was dead. There were moments in life when, as a scientist, you could only stand by. The American colleagues were confronted with experiences you simply cannot get from reading books. The directness of our people, their openness, their warmth, moved everyone. People saw the Americans as saviors. One woman spoke very emotionally to Dr Lee and said, “Tell your president how difficult it is for us.” She believed he had come directly on his orders. They looked at them almost like saints. For them, this was the fulfillment of their last hope, the final chance to do something, to find their loved ones. Families, opened up to them in a very personal way and we would translate for them. They confided in them as if they were their closest ones. There were no barriers between them.

Leskur Staničić: What did the identification process in Split look like?

Anđelinović: In the autopsy room, on the tables, next to each disfigured body, we would lay out personal items that might help with identification, things we had found with the remains. Then the families would come in and look at them. In some cases, relatives recognized their loved ones by a sweater their mother had knitted. By a watch, a rosary, even socks.

PDF page 28

Inače nismo imali zubne kartone, s kojima Amerikanci postižu velike rezultate. Kada bi nam članovi obitelji rekli o kome se radi, mi bismo dalje provjeravali, određivali antropološke karakteristike – visinu, dob, spol, nastojali smo utvrditi uzrok smrti koliko je to bilo moguće, nakon čega smo uzimali uzorke DNA.

Leskur Staničić: Je li Vam se neki slučaj posebno urezao u pamćenje?

Anđelinović: U ratu mi se pokazalo da je najveća ljubav ona između sestre i brata. Jedna je žena često dolazila u obdukcijsku salu,
